Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Route a concern without unnecessary circulation

In the lesson's safeguarding case, a manager discusses routing a volunteer's concern with the safeguarding focal point; no identifying details are included in this training exchange.

  1. Program manager: A volunteer raised a safeguarding concern. Could you help me use our established reporting process without unnecessary circulation?
  2. Safeguarding focal point: Yes. Please use the designated reporting channel and keep identifying details out of this general conversation.
  3. Program manager: Should I question other volunteers first so the incident report contains a more complete explanation?
  4. Safeguarding focal point: Don't start an informal investigation. Follow the reporting process and distinguish what was reported from what you observed.
  5. Program manager: Understood. I want to handle the information carefully without promising confidentiality beyond what our process allows.
  6. Safeguarding focal point: Exactly. Use the approved explanation of confidentiality and the survivor-centered approach specified by our organization.
  7. Program manager: Could you confirm the secure route now, so I don't delay by guessing where the concern belongs?
  8. Safeguarding focal point: I'll direct you to the established channel and confirm how the authorized team acknowledges receipt.
  9. Program manager: I'll provide the relevant information there and avoid adding assumptions about what happened.
  10. Safeguarding focal point: Good. Keep the next action focused on appropriate reporting, not deciding the allegation yourself.
A concern sent to a shared inbox

Separate fictional case: a fictional nonprofit receives a safeguarding message in a general project inbox; the coordinator asks its designated focal point for the approved transfer route.

  1. Project coordinator: A safeguarding message reached our shared inbox. Please confirm its approved transfer route.
  2. Safeguarding focal point: Use our established handling process. Don't forward it to the whole project team to find an available reviewer.
  3. Project coordinator: I won't repeat the identifying details here. Should I create my own summary and discard the original?
  4. Safeguarding focal point: No. Follow the organization's instructions for preserving and routing the original information; don't improvise deletion or retention.
  5. Project coordinator: Could you help me identify who acknowledges receipt, so I can confirm the concern hasn't been lost?
  6. Safeguarding focal point: Yes. I'll connect you with the designated reporting route and its acknowledgment process.
  7. Project coordinator: If a colleague asks what the message says, I'll explain that access is limited to the appropriate handling process.
  8. Safeguarding focal point: Good. Confidentiality means careful information handling, not a promise that nobody else will ever need to know.
  9. Project coordinator: I'll complete the authorized transfer and report the routing status without discussing the substance in this inbox.
  10. Safeguarding focal point: That keeps the request actionable and protects the information from unnecessary circulation.
An update request without case details

Separate fictional case: a manager knows a safeguarding concern was submitted through the approved channel but has no acknowledgment reference; they ask only about receipt, not findings.

  1. Volunteer manager: Could you confirm receipt of the concern submitted through our reporting channel? I don't have an acknowledgment reference.
  2. Safeguarding coordinator: I can check the routing status through the authorized process. Please don't repeat the case details here.
  3. Volunteer manager: Thank you. I'm not asking for investigative findings, only whether the report reached the responsible team.
  4. Safeguarding coordinator: That distinction matters. An acknowledgment confirms receipt, not a conclusion or permission to share information more widely.
  5. Volunteer manager: A team leader wants an update for the whole group. Should I summarize the allegation for them?
  6. Safeguarding coordinator: Use our established communication guidance instead. Don't assume a general team update needs the allegation's details.
  7. Volunteer manager: Understood. Could you tell me which approved wording applies when colleagues ask about the reporting status?
  8. Safeguarding coordinator: I'll obtain that guidance and confirm what can appropriately be communicated within your role.
  9. Volunteer manager: I'll wait for the authorized wording and avoid promising a particular investigation outcome or completion date.
  10. Safeguarding coordinator: Good. I'll focus this follow-up on receipt and the appropriate communication route.

Identify the appropriate reporting steps

Volunteer Hana reports what a participant told her; she did not witness the event. The usual lead is implicated. The fictional policy provides an independent alternate contact and a secure reporting channel. Immediate danger requires the relevant emergency route.

Answer and explanation

Hana describes information from the participant rather than an event she personally witnessed.

Answer and explanation

The stated policy provides this alternative for a concern involving the normal recipient.

3. Manager: "We send necessary details through the ___."

Answer and explanation

B. secure reporting channel

Sensitive information belongs in the designated channel rather than a general conversation.

4. Hana: "Immediate danger requires the relevant ___."

Urgent safety needs must not wait for an ordinary handover or a particular colleague.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 05: Safeguarding and Incident Reporting
Original fictional case: Volunteer Elena tells manager Sam that a participant reported a worker threatening to withdraw support unless the participant did a private favor. Elena did not witness the interaction and does not know whether there is an immediate danger. The fictional organization requires concerns to be passed promptly through its secure safeguarding route, without waiting for proof. Its policy provides an independent alternate contact if the usual lead is implicated or unavailable. Immediate danger requires the relevant emergency route without delay. Sam must listen, clarify immediate safety needs, record the account accurately, and avoid investigating or confronting the worker.
Language workshop: Receive the concern without conducting your own investigation
Communication goal: Respond calmly to a concern, distinguish reported information from direct observation, and explain the designated reporting route without promising secrecy.
Language guidance: Elena can report exactly what the participant told her while stating that she did not witness the interaction. That distinction does not make the concern unimportant. It preserves the source of the information without declaring the allegation proved or disproved.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for bringing this concern to me. / Is anyone in immediate danger or in need of urgent assistance? / Did you witness the interaction, or are you describing what you were told? / Please use the words you remember, without filling in missing details. / We do not yet know whether the reported event occurred as described. / You do not need to investigate before raising the concern. / I cannot promise secrecy; I need to use the designated reporting route. / I will share the necessary information with the people who need to act. / I will pass this through the approved secure channel promptly. / If the usual lead is implicated or unavailable, I will use the policy's alternate contact. / Do not approach the worker to test the account yourself. / The authorized process will determine the next fact-finding steps. / I will separate what you observed from what the participant reported. / Immediate danger requires the relevant emergency route without delay. / I cannot predict the outcome, but I can explain the next reporting step. / I will confirm that the designated recipient has received the concern.
Vocabulary: safeguarding: Organizational work to prevent and respond to harm, abuse, and exploitation. / concern: Information suggesting a possible problem that needs appropriate attention. / disclosure: A person's communication of information about an experience or event. / allegation: A reported claim not yet established through the relevant process. / direct observation: Something a person personally saw or heard. / reported account: Information communicated by another person. / immediate danger: A present threat requiring urgent action through the appropriate route. / designated lead: The person assigned responsibility for a defined safeguarding function. / alternate contact: A specified substitute reporting contact under the policy. / reporting route: The approved channel through which a concern is passed. / confidentiality: Limits on access to information under the applicable arrangements. / need-to-know basis: Sharing information only with those requiring it for the relevant task. / secure channel: An approved communication method protecting sensitive information appropriately. / factual record: An account separating known facts, sources, and uncertainties. / verbatim wording: The exact words used by a speaker. / inference: A conclusion drawn beyond what was directly stated or observed. / retaliation: Harmful treatment in response to a report or other protected action. / power imbalance: Unequal influence or control between people or groups. / coercion: Pressure or threats used to influence someone's actions. / professional boundary: A limit defining appropriate conduct in a work relationship. / support referral: Connection to an appropriate source of assistance. / investigation: An authorized process to establish relevant facts. / information minimization: Limiting information to what is necessary for the stated purpose. / acknowledged receipt: Confirmation that the designated recipient received the concern.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 calmly, preserving the reported source and any uncertainty. Swap roles for turns 11-20, stressing the secure route and independent alternate contact. Complete Hana's exchange below; check all four answers before reading the reporting sequence aloud.
Partner: Elena: Volunteer reporting a concern / Sam: Receiving manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Volunteer Hana reports what a participant told her; she did not witness the event. The usual lead is implicated. The fictional policy provides an independent alternate contact and a secure reporting channel. Immediate danger requires the relevant emergency route.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: I also want to know what I can say if the participant asks what will happen to the worker.
Sam: Do not predict the outcome. Explain the next reporting route and appropriate support arrangements. We can be clear about the process without promising a finding, a sanction, or information we may not be able to share.
Elena: I will keep the account accurate and avoid discussing it with other volunteers. Please let me know that the concern reaches the right recipient.
Sam: I will obtain acknowledged receipt. That confirms the handover, not the outcome. If new immediate safety information emerges, use the appropriate urgent route rather than waiting for a routine update.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Volunteer Management and TrainingAn enthusiastic offer is not a driving authorization+

Your goal

Acknowledge a volunteer contribution while maintaining role boundaries, explaining the approval process, and arranging authorized cover.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

An enthusiastic offer is not a driving authorization

Food-support volunteer Luis is approved for packing and stock counting. A driver becomes unavailable before a delivery, and Luis offers to drive the organization van. Coordinator Grace has no record that Luis has completed the organization-specific driving checks, assessment, or authorization. His ordinary driving experience does not replace those requirements. The fictional policy allows only currently authorized drivers to use the van. Grace must keep Luis within his approved role, contact the transport lead for authorized cover, and explain how he can request assessment for a future driving role without promising approval.

Luis: Packing volunteer / Grace: Volunteer coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which duties are currently approved for Luis?

Answer and explanation

D. Packing and stock counting

The brief defines Luis's approved role as packing and stock counting only.

2. What does the fictional policy require for van use?

Answer and explanation

B. Current driver authorization

The policy restricts van use to currently authorized drivers, regardless of enthusiasm or schedule pressure.

3. What can Grace offer Luis now?

Answer and explanation

A. Continue approved duties and request assessment for a future driving role.

Grace can explain the development route while preserving the present authorization boundary.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Experience, training completion, demonstrated competence, and authorization are related but distinct. Luis may have driving experience without holding permission to use this organization van. Finishing a course also need not complete every assessment or approval requirement.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
role description
A statement of duties, responsibilities, and limits.review the role description
volunteer agreement
A document setting out the organization's and volunteer's arrangements.clarify the volunteer agreement
induction
Initial orientation to the organization and relevant work.complete volunteer induction
onboarding
The process of preparing someone to join an organization or role.improve volunteer onboarding
scope of role
The activities and responsibilities a role includes.stay within the scope of role
role boundary
A limit separating authorized duties from other work.maintain a role boundary
screening
Relevant checks used to assess suitability under the applicable process.complete role-specific screening
competence
Demonstrated ability to perform a task appropriately.assess competence
training completion
Finishing a specified training activity.record training completion
competency assessment
Evaluation of whether someone can perform specified tasks appropriately.arrange a competency assessment
authorization
Permission from the relevant authority to perform a task.verify current authorization
supervision
Oversight and support for someone's work.provide appropriate supervision
refresher training
Further training to maintain or update relevant knowledge and skills.schedule refresher training
cover
A replacement arrangement when the usual person is unavailable.arrange authorized cover
roster
A schedule assigning people to duties or shifts.update the volunteer roster
shift coordinator
The person organizing duties during a particular work period.contact the shift coordinator
transport lead
The person responsible for coordinating the relevant transport function.notify the transport lead
task allocation
Assignment of work to people within relevant limits.confirm task allocation
escalation route
The designated path for an issue needing another level of responsibility.use the escalation route
availability notice
Information about when someone can or cannot work.send an availability notice
reasonable adjustment
An appropriate change considered under applicable needs, duties, and policy.discuss reasonable adjustments
feedback conversation
A discussion about performance, experience, or improvement.arrange a feedback conversation
role expansion
A proposed increase in someone's authorized responsibilities.request a role expansion
authorization register
A record showing who holds the relevant permission and its status.check the authorization register

An urgent delivery does not expand the authority of Grace or Luis. Grace should explain the limit, contact the transport lead, and keep useful approved work moving. The future assessment route is an opportunity to apply, not a promised authorization.

C Notice the language

Appreciation and boundaries can coexist

A volunteer may interpret a refusal as distrust or ingratitude. Recognize the offer and explain the role-based requirement without making the person defend their character. Give an appropriate current task and a clear route to request a broader role later.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Recognize the offer
Thank you for offering to cover the delivery.
State the current role
Your approved duties are packing and stock counting.
Separate experience from permission
Your driving experience does not establish authorization for this van.
Name the policy limit
Only currently authorized drivers may use the organization vehicle.
Avoid a personal judgment
This is a role requirement, not a judgment about your intentions.
Check the record
I need to verify the current authorization register.
Keep the task bounded
Please continue with the approved packing work.
Assign the escalation
I will contact the transport lead for authorized cover.
Avoid a shortcut
We cannot skip the required checks because the delivery is urgent.
Explain the development route
You can request assessment for a future driving role.
Distinguish training and approval
Completing training is not automatically the final authorization.
Avoid a guarantee
I cannot promise the assessment result or approval date.
Check support needs
We can discuss the arrangements needed for the assessment process.
Clarify supervision
Who will provide the required oversight for the approved task?
Keep the roster accurate
Update the roster only after the authorized arrangement is confirmed.
Close respectfully
Your help is valuable, and we need to use it within the agreed role.
6 language and precision notes
Thank you for offering
Recognizes a contribution before explaining the boundary.
Approved duties
Names the work currently permitted.
Does not establish
Separates one credential from a different permission.
Not a judgment
Prevents a process requirement sounding like a personal accusation.
Can request
Offers a route without promising the outcome.
Only after
Keeps the record aligned with the actual authorization.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response respects both Luis and the policy?

Answer and explanation

D. Thank you; I will seek authorized cover while you continue the approved packing work.

The response acknowledges the offer and keeps both the delivery and Luis's work within the stated role limits.

2. What does finishing a training course establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

C. Completion of that course, not necessarily final authorization

Training completion records one learning step; it does not establish an assessment result, interim permission, or roster authorization.

3. Who should Grace contact for replacement transport?

Answer and explanation

B. The transport lead

The brief assigns the authorized-cover route to the transport lead rather than the volunteer or an unrelated party.

4. Which future-role statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. You may request assessment, but approval is not guaranteed.

A request starts the specified development process without determining its result.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

An enthusiastic offer is not a driving authorization

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Luis: Packing volunteer / Grace: Volunteer coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • authorization
  • transport lead
  • Training completion
  • role expansion
  • competency assessment
  • role boundary
  • authorization register
  • scope of role
  • task allocation
  • roster
  1. Luis
    We are short a driver, and the boxes are nearly ready. I have driven vans before. Could I cover this delivery, or do you need to check my role first?
  2. Grace
    Thank you for offering. Your current 1 covers packing and stock counting. I do not have a record that you are authorized to drive our organization vehicle.
  3. Luis
    I understand that it is outside my usual tasks, but I have plenty of ordinary driving experience. Is that not enough for a short trip?
  4. Grace
    Experience is relevant, but the 2 is separate. Our policy permits only currently authorized drivers to use the van, even for a short delivery or when the normal driver is unavailable.
  5. Luis
    I see. I was trying to keep the delivery moving, not put you on the spot. What can I do now that will actually help?
  6. Grace
    Your offer is helpful. Maintaining this 3 is not a judgment about your intentions. We need to arrange the delivery through the appropriate person while keeping your approved work moving.
  7. Luis
    Is there a list you can check, or does the decision depend on who happens to be coordinating the shift?
  8. Grace
    We use the 4. I need a current record, not an assumption based on familiarity or confidence. Nothing in your current role record establishes the required driving approval.
  9. Luis
    Then who can arrange another driver? I can finish the packing while you find out.
  10. Grace
    I will contact the 5 for authorized cover. We may need to revise the delivery arrangement, but urgency does not give either of us permission to bypass the requirement.
  11. Luis
    I would like to help with driving in future. Could we make that part of my role instead of having this problem again?
  12. Grace
    You can request a 6. I can explain the organization-specific checks, assessment, and decision process. I cannot promise that the request will be approved or give you an approval date today.
  13. Luis
    If I complete the training, would that mean I can start driving on the next shift?
  14. Grace
    Not necessarily. 7 is one step. We must also establish which assessment and approval requirements remain, rather than treating a course certificate as permission to undertake every task.
  15. Luis
    So the assessment is about demonstrating the required ability, and the final authorization records permission to take on the duty.
  16. Grace
    Exactly. The 8 checks the relevant task requirements. The process should also explain what support or appropriate adjustments can be discussed, without promising a particular result.
  17. Luis
    For today, I will stay with packing and stock counting. Please tell me if there is another task within my current role that would help.
  18. Grace
    I will confirm the 9 for this shift. That lets us use your help effectively while the transport lead handles the replacement arrangement and any necessary schedule change.
  19. Luis
    Once another driver is confirmed, I can help the team prepare the correctly labeled packages for collection.
  20. Grace
    Good. We will update the 10 after the authorized cover is confirmed. We can also arrange a separate conversation about your interest in the driving role, without confusing that future request with today's permission.

Answers and explanations
  1. scope of role Turn 2. Luis is approved for packing and stock counting; driving is outside those current duties.
  2. authorization Turn 4. Authorization is current organizational permission; ordinary driving experience does not replace the required approval.
  3. role boundary Turn 6. The boundary limits permitted duties without judging the volunteer's intentions or willingness to help.
  4. authorization register Turn 8. The register establishes current permission rather than relying on an improvised judgment of confidence.
  5. transport lead Turn 10. The transport lead is responsible for arranging authorized cover; Luis cannot approve himself.
  6. role expansion Turn 12. Role expansion requests broader duties but requires the relevant assessment and approval before permission.
  7. Training completion Turn 14. Finishing training establishes course completion; separate assessment and authorization requirements may still remain.
  8. competency assessment Turn 16. The assessment checks demonstrated ability against task requirements, rather than merely recording training attendance.
  9. task allocation Turn 18. Task allocation keeps Luis working within his approved duties while transport cover is arranged.
  10. roster Turn 20. The roster should record confirmed authorized cover, not an offer from an unapproved driver.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Luis: We are short a driver, and the boxes are nearly ready. I have driven vans before. Could I cover this delivery, or do you need to check my role first?
  2. Grace: Thank you for offering. Your current scope of role covers packing and stock counting. I do not have a record that you are authorized to drive our organization vehicle.
  3. Luis: I understand that it is outside my usual tasks, but I have plenty of ordinary driving experience. Is that not enough for a short trip?
  4. Grace: Experience is relevant, but the authorization is separate. Our policy permits only currently authorized drivers to use the van, even for a short delivery or when the normal driver is unavailable.
  5. Luis: I see. I was trying to keep the delivery moving, not put you on the spot. What can I do now that will actually help?
  6. Grace: Your offer is helpful. Maintaining this role boundary is not a judgment about your intentions. We need to arrange the delivery through the appropriate person while keeping your approved work moving.
  7. Luis: Is there a list you can check, or does the decision depend on who happens to be coordinating the shift?
  8. Grace: We use the authorization register. I need a current record, not an assumption based on familiarity or confidence. Nothing in your current role record establishes the required driving approval.
  9. Luis: Then who can arrange another driver? I can finish the packing while you find out.
  10. Grace: I will contact the transport lead for authorized cover. We may need to revise the delivery arrangement, but urgency does not give either of us permission to bypass the requirement.
  11. Luis: I would like to help with driving in future. Could we make that part of my role instead of having this problem again?
  12. Grace: You can request a role expansion. I can explain the organization-specific checks, assessment, and decision process. I cannot promise that the request will be approved or give you an approval date today.
  13. Luis: If I complete the training, would that mean I can start driving on the next shift?
  14. Grace: Not necessarily. Training completion is one step. We must also establish which assessment and approval requirements remain, rather than treating a course certificate as permission to undertake every task.
  15. Luis: So the assessment is about demonstrating the required ability, and the final authorization records permission to take on the duty.
  16. Grace: Exactly. The competency assessment checks the relevant task requirements. The process should also explain what support or appropriate adjustments can be discussed, without promising a particular result.
  17. Luis: For today, I will stay with packing and stock counting. Please tell me if there is another task within my current role that would help.
  18. Grace: I will confirm the task allocation for this shift. That lets us use your help effectively while the transport lead handles the replacement arrangement and any necessary schedule change.
  19. Luis: Once another driver is confirmed, I can help the team prepare the correctly labeled packages for collection.
  20. Grace: Good. We will update the roster after the authorized cover is confirmed. We can also arrange a separate conversation about your interest in the driving role, without confusing that future request with today's permission.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Enthusiasm beyond the agreed role

Separate fictional case aligned with the lesson: a reception volunteer trained to book appointments offers to conduct eligibility interviews alone; those interviews belong to the trained program team.

  1. Volunteer supervisor: You offered to conduct eligibility interviews alone, but your agreed reception role covers appointment booking.
  2. Reception volunteer: I wanted to help with the queue. I thought listening to an interview meant I could conduct one.
  3. Volunteer supervisor: Observing helps you learn, but it does not establish the training or authorization this task requires.
  4. Reception volunteer: I see. I could give visitors an inaccurate impression that I am making an official assessment.
  5. Volunteer supervisor: Exactly. Please continue booking appointments and refer eligibility questions to the trained program team.
  6. Reception volunteer: Can I still ask about developing my volunteer role without taking on those interviews now?
  7. Volunteer supervisor: Certainly. We can discuss appropriate training and supervision with the program lead, without promising a role change.
  8. Reception volunteer: For this shift, I will manage bookings and ask the program team to handle interview requests.
  9. Volunteer supervisor: Good. If the queue grows, tell me so we can coordinate support within the agreed arrangements.
  10. Reception volunteer: I will. I will not conduct interviews independently, and I will raise the queue issue with you.
A helpful answer that went beyond training

Separate fictional case: a reception volunteer answered a visitor's eligibility question despite being trained only to explain appointment booking; no eligibility decision is established.

  1. Volunteer supervisor: You told the visitor they were eligible, although your current role covers appointment booking, not eligibility assessment.
  2. Reception volunteer: I recognized one condition from the leaflet and assumed that was enough. I should have checked.
  3. Volunteer supervisor: That answer may create an expectation of approval. The program team needs to assess the relevant information.
  4. Reception volunteer: Should I contact the visitor myself and say they are not eligible instead?
  5. Volunteer supervisor: No. That would make another unsupported decision. I will arrange an appropriate correction with the program team.
  6. Reception volunteer: Understood. What should I say when another visitor asks whether meeting one condition guarantees approval?
  7. Volunteer supervisor: Explain that the authorized team assesses eligibility, and offer to help with the appointment process.
  8. Reception volunteer: I can say, I can help you book, but the program team must answer the eligibility question.
  9. Volunteer supervisor: Exactly. Let's practice two more examples together before you return to the reception desk.
  10. Reception volunteer: Yes. I will keep my answers within booking and ask for support when a question goes beyond it.
A shift handover missing the unresolved request

Separate fictional case: a volunteer's handover omitted an unresolved accessible-appointment request, so the receiving coordinator could not follow up without asking the visitor again.

  1. Shift supervisor: Your handover listed completed tasks but omitted the accessible-appointment request the next coordinator needed.
  2. Reception volunteer: I thought the handover covered only finished work. I did not realize pending requests belonged there too.
  3. Shift supervisor: Without that action, the coordinator had to ask the visitor to explain the request again.
  4. Reception volunteer: I understand the effect. Should I include every detail from the visitor's conversation next time?
  5. Shift supervisor: No. Use the approved record and share only what the receiving role needs to take the next action.
  6. Reception volunteer: Then I should identify the outstanding appointment request and ask who will accept the follow-up.
  7. Shift supervisor: Exactly. End the handover with a spoken check of pending actions and the person responsible.
  8. Reception volunteer: For my next shift change, I will ask the coordinator to confirm the outstanding action before I leave.
  9. Shift supervisor: Good. I will observe that handover and help if the responsibility or information is unclear.
  10. Reception volunteer: Thank you. I will distinguish completed work from requests still needing attention and confirm the receiving owner.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An enthusiastic offer is not a driving authorization
  1. Read turns 1-10, pairing appreciation with a clear present-role limit.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20 and distinguish training, assessment, and authorization.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep another volunteer within an approved role

Maya is approved for reception duties, not home visits. She offers to visit a client alone when a colleague cancels. The coordinator must seek authorized cover and can explain the future role-assessment process, without guaranteeing approval.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "Your current approved work is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. reception duties

The stated role permits reception work, not an unapproved independent home visit.

2. Maya: "Today's replacement arrangement needs ___."

Answer and explanation

D. authorized cover

The canceled visit must be addressed through appropriately authorized staff or volunteers.

3. Coordinator: "A future expanded role requires the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. role-assessment process

A request for broader duties must follow the stated assessment route before permission is assumed.

4. Maya: "Applying does not mean that approval is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. guaranteed

The coordinator can explain the process but cannot promise its eventual outcome.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary

2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Volunteer Management and Training
Original fictional case: Food-support volunteer Luis is approved for packing and stock counting. A driver becomes unavailable before a delivery, and Luis offers to drive the organization van. Coordinator Grace has no record that Luis has completed the organization-specific driving checks, assessment, or authorization. His ordinary driving experience does not replace those requirements. The fictional policy allows only currently authorized drivers to use the van. Grace must keep Luis within his approved role, contact the transport lead for authorized cover, and explain how he can request assessment for a future driving role without promising approval.
Language workshop: An enthusiastic offer is not a driving authorization
Communication goal: Acknowledge a volunteer contribution while maintaining role boundaries, explaining the approval process, and arranging authorized cover.
Language guidance: Experience, training completion, demonstrated competence, and authorization are related but distinct. Luis may have driving experience without holding permission to use this organization van. Finishing a course also need not complete every assessment or approval requirement.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for offering to cover the delivery. / Your approved duties are packing and stock counting. / Your driving experience does not establish authorization for this van. / Only currently authorized drivers may use the organization vehicle. / This is a role requirement, not a judgment about your intentions. / I need to verify the current authorization register. / Please continue with the approved packing work. / I will contact the transport lead for authorized cover. / We cannot skip the required checks because the delivery is urgent. / You can request assessment for a future driving role. / Completing training is not automatically the final authorization. / I cannot promise the assessment result or approval date. / We can discuss the arrangements needed for the assessment process. / Who will provide the required oversight for the approved task? / Update the roster only after the authorized arrangement is confirmed. / Your help is valuable, and we need to use it within the agreed role.
Vocabulary: role description: A statement of duties, responsibilities, and limits. / volunteer agreement: A document setting out the organization's and volunteer's arrangements. / induction: Initial orientation to the organization and relevant work. / onboarding: The process of preparing someone to join an organization or role. / scope of role: The activities and responsibilities a role includes. / role boundary: A limit separating authorized duties from other work. / screening: Relevant checks used to assess suitability under the applicable process. / competence: Demonstrated ability to perform a task appropriately. / training completion: Finishing a specified training activity. / competency assessment: Evaluation of whether someone can perform specified tasks appropriately. / authorization: Permission from the relevant authority to perform a task. / supervision: Oversight and support for someone's work. / refresher training: Further training to maintain or update relevant knowledge and skills. / cover: A replacement arrangement when the usual person is unavailable. / roster: A schedule assigning people to duties or shifts. / shift coordinator: The person organizing duties during a particular work period. / transport lead: The person responsible for coordinating the relevant transport function. / task allocation: Assignment of work to people within relevant limits. / escalation route: The designated path for an issue needing another level of responsibility. / availability notice: Information about when someone can or cannot work. / reasonable adjustment: An appropriate change considered under applicable needs, duties, and policy. / feedback conversation: A discussion about performance, experience, or improvement. / role expansion: A proposed increase in someone's authorized responsibilities. / authorization register: A record showing who holds the relevant permission and its status.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Advocacy, Public Messaging, and NeutralityAn alarming headline changes the meaning of an old survey+

Your goal

Challenge an unsupported public claim, preserve the original population and time frame, and distinguish policy advocacy from party endorsement.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

An alarming headline changes the meaning of an old survey

Fundraiser Maya drafts a headline: one in three local children is hungry tonight. Communications officer Theo traces the number to a fictional 2018 regional survey of 80 self-selected adult respondents. Twenty-seven reported concern about having enough food during the previous month. The survey did not measure current hunger among local children, and its representativeness is unknown. The organization permits evidence-based policy advocacy but has its own nonpartisan rule against endorsing parties. Maya and Theo must remove or accurately qualify the statistic, retain a clear mission-focused request, and avoid unsupported urgency or partisan endorsement.

Maya: Fundraiser / Theo: Communications officer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Who answered the original survey?

Answer and explanation

A. Eighty self-selected adult respondents in the region

The source population is the stated self-selected adult respondent group, not local children.

2. What did 27 respondents report?

Answer and explanation

D. Concern about enough food during the previous month

The original question concerned respondents' past-month food concerns, not the headline's different population and time frame.

3. What does the organization's own policy allow?

Answer and explanation

A. Evidence-based policy advocacy without party endorsement

The fictional policy distinguishes advocacy about issues from endorsing political parties.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Twenty-seven of eighty is 33.75%, roughly one third of those respondents. That arithmetic does not validate the headline. Adults are not children, the region is not necessarily the local area, past-month concern is not measured hunger tonight, and the sample is not established as representative.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
advocacy
Communication seeking change in policy, practice, or public attention.support evidence-based advocacy
public messaging
Communication intended for an external audience.review public messaging
fundraising appeal
A request for financial or other support.prepare a fundraising appeal
claim substantiation
Evidence supporting what a statement asserts.check claim substantiation
source attribution
Identifying where information came from.provide source attribution
survey population
The defined group a survey concerns.describe the survey population
respondent
A person who provides answers to a survey.count survey respondents
self-selection
Entry into a sample through people's own decision to participate.disclose self-selection
representativeness
How well a sample reflects the relevant wider population.assess representativeness
denominator
The total quantity used as the base of a proportion.identify the denominator
time frame
The period to which a measurement or claim refers.preserve the time frame
question wording
The exact phrasing used to collect a survey response.check question wording
historical finding
A result referring to an earlier period.label a historical finding
extrapolation
Extending a finding beyond the observations or population measured.avoid unsupported extrapolation
qualifier
Wording that specifies the limits or conditions of a statement.add a material qualifier
headline
The prominent title summarizing a message.revise the headline
call to action
A clear request for what an audience should do next.state the call to action
nonpartisan
Not supporting or opposing political parties under the relevant meaning or policy.maintain a nonpartisan position
party endorsement
An expression of support for a political party.avoid party endorsement
policy position
An organization's stated view on a public issue or proposed measure.explain the policy position
informed consent
Agreement based on relevant information about the proposed use or action.obtain informed consent
dignity
Respect for a person's worth and agency.preserve participant dignity
editorial approval
Authorization of material through the relevant publication process.obtain editorial approval
correction notice
A statement identifying and correcting published misinformation.issue a correction notice

Naming a source does not repair a claim that changes its meaning. If the historical finding is used, its population, method, date, and actual question need visible context. Removing an unsuitable statistic may produce a clearer appeal than attaching a long footnote to a misleading headline.

C Notice the language

Urgency does not require distortion

A fundraising message can be forceful without changing who was measured or when. Separate the emotional force of the issue from the strength of the evidence. A precise correction can protect the mission and the dignity of the people described, not merely reduce legal or reputational exposure.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Pause publication
This headline needs an evidence check before release.
Trace the figure
What is the original source for one in three?
Name the population
The survey involved eighty self-selected adult respondents.
Restore the date
The finding comes from a regional survey in two thousand eighteen.
Restore the question
Respondents reported concern about enough food during the previous month.
Reject the population change
That does not establish current hunger among local children.
Keep arithmetic separate
The proportion is roughly one third, but the headline still changes the claim.
Question extrapolation
We do not know whether these respondents represent the wider population.
Avoid attribution as a shield
Adding a source name does not make the revised claim accurate.
Choose a clear correction
Remove the statistic or state the historical finding with its essential limits.
Retain the request
We can make a clear call to action without inventing a current prevalence figure.
Explain advocacy
We can argue for a policy change using appropriately supported evidence.
Maintain the policy boundary
Our nonpartisan rule does not permit endorsing a party.
Respect story ownership
Check informed consent and the intended use before publishing someone's story.
Protect dignity
Describe people as individuals with agency, not as fundraising props.
Close the review
Approve wording that is accurate in the headline as well as the supporting text.
6 language and precision notes
Original source
Directs the check to the underlying measurement.
That does not establish
Rejects an unsupported inference without denying the issue exists.
Roughly one third
Describes the calculation, not wider representativeness.
Essential limits
Identifies context that changes how a result should be understood.
Without inventing
Keeps the appeal strong while respecting the evidence boundary.
Our rule
Refers to this organization's policy rather than a universal rule for NGOs.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement accurately reflects the source?

Answer and explanation

A. In the 2018 regional survey, 27 of 80 self-selected adult respondents reported past-month food concerns.

The accurate statement preserves the source's date, respondents, quantity, and actual measured concern.

2. Does adding a citation rescue the original headline?

Answer and explanation

C. No; attribution does not correct its changed population and time frame.

A citation identifies a source but cannot make a claim faithful when its meaning has been altered.

3. Which advocacy sentence fits the stated nonpartisan rule?

Answer and explanation

B. We support the proposed access reform on the evidence, without endorsing a party.

The fictional rule allows issue-based advocacy while withholding party endorsement.

4. What should accompany use of a participant story?

Answer and explanation

A. The relevant consent and dignity checks for its intended publication

Responsible publication requires the relevant consent and respectful representation, not automatic permission derived from service use.

E Conversations

An alarming headline changes the meaning of an old survey

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Maya: Fundraiser / Theo: Communications officer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • question wording
  • source attribution
  • call to action
  • editorial approval
  • policy position
  • denominator
  • informed consent
  • time frame
  • extrapolation
  • self-selection
  1. Maya
    Before this appeal goes out, I need the source behind one in three local children is hungry tonight. It is our lead claim, and I cannot trace it from the draft.
  2. Theo
    I traced the 1 to a regional survey from two thousand eighteen. It involved eighty adult respondents, not a survey of local children or a current count of hunger.
  3. Maya
    The number came from twenty-seven responses out of eighty. That is roughly one third, so is the calculation at least correct?
  4. Theo
    The 2 supports that approximate proportion among respondents. But correct arithmetic cannot fix a change in who was measured, what they were asked, or when the finding applies.
  5. Maya
    Can you read me the actual question? We have shortened this copy three times. I suspect we have shortened away something essential.
  6. Theo
    The 3 concerned whether respondents had worried about having enough food during the previous month. It did not establish that children were hungry on a particular night.
  7. Maya
    And we cannot turn a finding from two thousand eighteen into a claim about tonight without current evidence.
  8. Theo
    Correct. The 4 is material to the meaning. We need to restore it if we use the result, rather than place an old date in a footnote under a current-sounding headline.
  9. Maya
    Do we know how the eighty people were selected? I do not want to imply that they represent every household in the region.
  10. Theo
    They joined through 5. We do not know whether they represent the wider population. The result describes those respondents, and the method limits how far we can extend it.
  11. Maya
    So adding the source name would not rescue the original headline. It would still make a different claim from the underlying finding.
  12. Theo
    Exactly. That would remain unsupported 6. Either remove the statistic or state the historical result with its essential limits. A citation is not permission to change the evidence.
  13. Maya
    A fully qualified historical finding may be too cumbersome for the headline. I would rather remove it and explain the work clearly.
  14. Theo
    We can retain a strong 7. State the support being requested and the work it will fund, using verified facts. Urgency should come from the actual need, not a claim we cannot substantiate.
  15. Maya
    The appeal also supports an access reform. A colleague thinks our nonpartisan rule means we cannot discuss any policy issue.
  16. Theo
    Our 8 can support an evidence-based reform. This organization's rule separates issue advocacy from endorsing parties; it does not require silence on every public policy question.
  17. Maya
    We also have a participant story available. I will check whether the proposed fundraising use is covered, rather than assume service participation gives permission.
  18. Theo
    Check 9 and the intended use, and preserve the person's dignity. Do not add an alarming detail simply because it might make the appeal more compelling.
  19. Maya
    I will remove the unsupported headline, keep a clear request, and send the revised wording through the publication process.
  20. Theo
    Then 10 can address the actual message as a whole. The headline, story, policy position, and supporting evidence should tell a consistent account that we can defend.

Answers and explanations
  1. source attribution Turn 2. Source attribution identifies the original survey but does not, by itself, make a changed or exaggerated headline accurate.
  2. denominator Turn 4. Denominator is the eighty respondents used to calculate the proportion; it is not the number of local children or all regional adults.
  3. question wording Turn 6. Question wording concerned enough food during the previous month, which differs from a measure of children experiencing hunger tonight.
  4. time frame Turn 8. Time frame limits the finding to the earlier survey and its past-month reference, not a current condition tonight.
  5. self-selection Turn 10. Self-selection means people chose to respond; representativeness of the wider population has not been established from this sample.
  6. extrapolation Turn 12. Extrapolation extends the respondent finding to a different population or present condition without evidence supporting that extension.
  7. call to action Turn 14. Call to action asks for support clearly without needing an invented or misrepresented current prevalence statistic.
  8. policy position Turn 16. Policy position states a view on an issue; the organization's stated rule allows evidence-based advocacy distinct from party endorsement.
  9. informed consent Turn 18. Informed consent concerns the proposed use of the person's story; receiving support does not automatically authorize fundraising publication.
  10. editorial approval Turn 20. Editorial approval should cover the corrected headline and supporting text, ensuring both communicate the verified claim consistently.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Maya: Before this appeal goes out, I need the source behind one in three local children is hungry tonight. It is our lead claim, and I cannot trace it from the draft.
  2. Theo: I traced the source attribution to a regional survey from two thousand eighteen. It involved eighty adult respondents, not a survey of local children or a current count of hunger.
  3. Maya: The number came from twenty-seven responses out of eighty. That is roughly one third, so is the calculation at least correct?
  4. Theo: The denominator supports that approximate proportion among respondents. But correct arithmetic cannot fix a change in who was measured, what they were asked, or when the finding applies.
  5. Maya: Can you read me the actual question? We have shortened this copy three times. I suspect we have shortened away something essential.
  6. Theo: The question wording concerned whether respondents had worried about having enough food during the previous month. It did not establish that children were hungry on a particular night.
  7. Maya: And we cannot turn a finding from two thousand eighteen into a claim about tonight without current evidence.
  8. Theo: Correct. The time frame is material to the meaning. We need to restore it if we use the result, rather than place an old date in a footnote under a current-sounding headline.
  9. Maya: Do we know how the eighty people were selected? I do not want to imply that they represent every household in the region.
  10. Theo: They joined through self-selection. We do not know whether they represent the wider population. The result describes those respondents, and the method limits how far we can extend it.
  11. Maya: So adding the source name would not rescue the original headline. It would still make a different claim from the underlying finding.
  12. Theo: Exactly. That would remain unsupported extrapolation. Either remove the statistic or state the historical result with its essential limits. A citation is not permission to change the evidence.
  13. Maya: A fully qualified historical finding may be too cumbersome for the headline. I would rather remove it and explain the work clearly.
  14. Theo: We can retain a strong call to action. State the support being requested and the work it will fund, using verified facts. Urgency should come from the actual need, not a claim we cannot substantiate.
  15. Maya: The appeal also supports an access reform. A colleague thinks our nonpartisan rule means we cannot discuss any policy issue.
  16. Theo: Our policy position can support an evidence-based reform. This organization's rule separates issue advocacy from endorsing parties; it does not require silence on every public policy question.
  17. Maya: We also have a participant story available. I will check whether the proposed fundraising use is covered, rather than assume service participation gives permission.
  18. Theo: Check informed consent and the intended use, and preserve the person's dignity. Do not add an alarming detail simply because it might make the appeal more compelling.
  19. Maya: I will remove the unsupported headline, keep a clear request, and send the revised wording through the publication process.
  20. Theo: Then editorial approval can address the actual message as a whole. The headline, story, policy position, and supporting evidence should tell a consistent account that we can defend.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A striking statistic without a source

The lesson's fundraising draft uses an alarming statistic with no source or date, and the campaign team wants to publish immediately.

  1. Campaign editor: The fundraising draft contains an alarming statistic, but its source and date are missing.
  2. Advocacy lead: It would give the campaign urgency. Can we keep it while someone looks for the reference?
  3. Campaign editor: We shouldn't publish it as established evidence before verification. Urgency doesn't establish accuracy or current relevance.
  4. Advocacy lead: Could we qualify it with reportedly rather than delay the whole message?
  5. Campaign editor: That word doesn't supply a source. We need to verify the claim or use wording supported by known information.
  6. Advocacy lead: Agreed. We should also consider whether the language respects the dignity of the people described.
  7. Campaign editor: Yes. An unsupported dramatic claim can misrepresent both their circumstances and our evidence.
  8. Advocacy lead: I'll ask the draft owner for the original source, date, and relevant population before using the figure.
  9. Campaign editor: I'll keep the statistic out of the publish-ready version until the appropriate review is complete.
  10. Advocacy lead: Then we can discuss an accurate campaign message without treating an unverified number as essential.
A participant quotation with an uncertain permission scope

Separate fictional case: a nonprofit has a participant quotation from an internal learning interview; permission for a public advocacy campaign has not been confirmed.

  1. Advocacy producer: This interview quotation is powerful, but we haven't confirmed permission to use it in the public campaign.
  2. Program communications lead: The participant agreed to the interview. Doesn't that establish permission to publish their words more widely?
  3. Advocacy producer: Not from the information we have. The interview agreement and public-use permission need to be checked.
  4. Program communications lead: Then I shouldn't describe the quotation as cleared just because it appears in our internal report.
  5. Advocacy producer: Correct. We also need to preserve context and dignity, rather than select wording only for emotional effect.
  6. Program communications lead: Could we anonymize it and assume that removes the permission question?
  7. Advocacy producer: No. Anonymization doesn't establish the scope of the original agreement. Use the organization's review process.
  8. Program communications lead: I'll ask the responsible colleague to verify permission and context without contacting the participant outside that process.
  9. Advocacy producer: Good. Meanwhile, keep the quotation out of the approved campaign assets and identify the unresolved question.
  10. Program communications lead: I'll do that and avoid claiming the quotation represents every participant's experience.
A campaign photograph from another year

Separate fictional case: an advocacy draft pairs a current event appeal with a clearly dated photograph from three years earlier; the caption omits the date.

  1. Visual communications editor: The photograph is from three years ago, but the current-event appeal doesn't identify that date.
  2. Campaign manager: Could readers assume it shows the present event, even though the photograph itself is genuine?
  3. Visual communications editor: Yes. A real image can still create an unsupported impression when the context is missing.
  4. Campaign manager: Then our issue isn't proving the photograph is fake; it's accurately describing what it shows.
  5. Visual communications editor: Exactly. We need the known date and context visible, alongside the usual permissions and dignity review.
  6. Campaign manager: Would calling it illustrative be enough without explaining that it comes from a different period?
  7. Visual communications editor: Let's avoid relying on an ambiguous label. The caption should not encourage a current-event interpretation we cannot support.
  8. Campaign manager: I'll ask for a contextually accurate asset or a clear caption, rather than suggest the image documents today's conditions.
  9. Visual communications editor: Good. Keep the campaign's verified claims separate from what the older photograph can establish.
  10. Campaign manager: We'll review the revised pairing before publication so the appeal remains accurate as well as compelling.

An alarming headline changes the meaning of an old survey

Keep a survey claim within its evidence

A 2020 survey of 60 self-selected adult service users found 18 reported transport difficulties in the previous month. It did not measure current difficulties among all city residents.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Editor: "The survey year is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 2020

The result is historical and cannot be relabeled as a current measurement.

2. Analyst: "The denominator is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 60 respondents

Sixty adult service users answered, not the entire city population.

3. Editor: "The proportion reporting difficulties is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 30%

Eighteen divided by sixty equals thirty percent of these respondents.

4. Analyst: "The question's reference period was the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. previous month

The stated survey question concerned the preceding month, not conditions today.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Vocabulary: advocacy: Communication seeking change in policy, practice, or public attention. / public messaging: Communication intended for an external audience. / fundraising appeal: A request for financial or other support. / claim substantiation: Evidence supporting what a statement asserts. / source attribution: Identifying where information came from. / survey population: The defined group a survey concerns. / respondent: A person who provides answers to a survey. / self-selection: Entry into a sample through people's own decision to participate. / representativeness: How well a sample reflects the relevant wider population. / denominator: The total quantity used as the base of a proportion. / time frame: The period to which a measurement or claim refers. / question wording: The exact phrasing used to collect a survey response. / historical finding: A result referring to an earlier period. / extrapolation: Extending a finding beyond the observations or population measured. / qualifier: Wording that specifies the limits or conditions of a statement. / headline: The prominent title summarizing a message. / call to action: A clear request for what an audience should do next. / nonpartisan: Not supporting or opposing political parties under the relevant meaning or policy. / party endorsement: An expression of support for a political party. / policy position: An organization's stated view on a public issue or proposed measure. / informed consent: Agreement based on relevant information about the proposed use or action. / dignity: Respect for a person's worth and agency. / editorial approval: Authorization of material through the relevant publication process. / correction notice: A statement identifying and correcting published misinformation.

Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Maya: We also have a participant story available. I will check whether the proposed fundraising use is covered, rather than assume service participation gives permission.
Theo: Check informed consent and the intended use, and preserve the person's dignity. Do not add an alarming detail simply because it might make the appeal more compelling.
Maya: I will remove the unsupported headline, keep a clear request, and send the revised wording through the publication process.
Theo: Then editorial approval can address the actual message as a whole. The headline, story, policy position, and supporting evidence should tell a consistent account that we can defend.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Advocacy, Public Messaging, and Neutrality
Original fictional case: Fundraiser Maya drafts a headline: one in three local children is hungry tonight. Communications officer Theo traces the number to a fictional 2018 regional survey of 80 self-selected adult respondents. Twenty-seven reported concern about having enough food during the previous month. The survey did not measure current hunger among local children, and its representativeness is unknown. The organization permits evidence-based policy advocacy but has its own nonpartisan rule against endorsing parties. Maya and Theo must remove or accurately qualify the statistic, retain a clear mission-focused request, and avoid unsupported urgency or partisan endorsement.
Language workshop: An alarming headline changes the meaning of an old survey
Communication goal: Challenge an unsupported public claim, preserve the original population and time frame, and distinguish policy advocacy from party endorsement.
Language guidance: Twenty-seven of eighty is 33.75%, roughly one third of those respondents. That arithmetic does not validate the headline. Adults are not children, the region is not necessarily the local area, past-month concern is not measured hunger tonight, and the sample is not established as representative.
